2c:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"But if you want to make AI output deterministic — parse it, validate it, and correct it in a loop until it converges — there is no alternative to structured output. Free-form text can’t be mechanically verified. Natural language can’t be compiled. Without structure, there’s no feedback loop, and without a feedback loop, there’s no guarantee. So we didn’t have the luxury of giving up on function calling. We had to make it work on the exact kind of complex, recursive schemas the industry had written off."}]

6c:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":[["$","a",null,{"href":"https://github.com/samchon/typia","target":"_blank","rel":"noreferrer","className":"x:focus-visible:nextra-focus x:text-primary-600 x:underline x:hover:no-underline x:decoration-from-font x:[text-underline-position:from-font]","children":["Typia",[" ",["$","svg",null,{"fill":"none","stroke":"currentColor","strokeLinecap":"round","strokeLinejoin":"round","strokeWidth":1.7,"viewBox":"0 0 24 24","height":"1em","className":"x:inline x:align-baseline x:shrink-0","children":[["$","path",null,{"d":"M7 17L17 7"}],["$","path",null,{"d":"M7 7h10v10"}]]}]]]}],". Making function calling reliable on recursive union types required going deeper than runtime libraries can reach. Runtime reflection can’t see TypeScript types — they’re erased at compilation. Zod-style schema builders choke on recursive unions (Appendix A.4 explains why). The only path was to operate at the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"compiler level"}]," itself — analyze types directly from source code and generate every piece of infrastructure from that single source of truth."]}]
6d:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["That’s what Typia is. A ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"compiler library"}]," that directly leverages the TypeScript compiler’s type analyzer to automatically generate JSON Schema, validators, parsers, and feedback generators at compile time. Define one type, and the compiler handles the rest. It’s the result of choosing to solve the problem at the deepest layer available, because every shallower approach hit a wall."]}]

89:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["There’s a critical difference. Most JSON repair tools (jsonrepair, dirty-json, LangChain’s ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"parse_partial_json"}],") operate at the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"string level"}]," — cleaning trailing commas, closing brackets, removing Markdown, then passing to ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"JSON.parse()"}],". A double-stringified value ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"\"{\\\"type\\\":\\\"card\\\"}\""}]," is already valid JSON (a string), so it passes through as-is. Without a schema, there’s no way to know it should be an object."]}]
8a:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Typia’s ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"parse()"}]," is different. It parses greedily while ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"referencing the schema"}]," generated by the compiler from types in Section 3.1. When it encounters a string where the schema expects an object, it recursively calls ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"parse()"}]," on that string. Parsing and coercion recursively call each other — a ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"schema-based recursive cycle"}]," — naturally unwinding layers of stringification. Double or triple."]}]
8b:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":[["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"parse()"}]," performs not just JSON recovery but also ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"schema-based type coercion"}]," simultaneously. LLMs frequently get types wrong — ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"\"42\""}]," (string) where ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"42"}]," (number) should be, ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"\"true\""}]," (string) where ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"true"}]," (boolean) should be. Simple casting doesn’t solve this. Whether ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"\"42\""}]," should become a number or stay a string depends entirely on the field’s schema, which was automatically generated by the Typia compiler from TypeScript types."]}]

c8:["$","ul",null,{"className":"x:[:is(ol,ul)_&]:my-[.75em] x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:list-disc x:ms-[1.5em]","children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"className":"x:my-[.5em]","children":"“Don’t create utility functions”"}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"className":"x:my-[.5em]","children":["“Don’t use the ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"any"}]," type”"]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"className":"x:my-[.5em]","children":"“Don’t create circular dependencies”"}],"\n"]}]
c9:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"“Don’t think of a pink elephant.” The first thing that comes to mind is a pink elephant. When you tell an LLM “don’t do X,” X gets placed at the center of attention. To avoid a forbidden pattern, the model must first recall that pattern, which paradoxically increases its generation probability. This is the essence of token prediction."}]
ca:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"Even knowing this, you can’t avoid prohibition rules in prompts. “Don’t do X” is the only way natural language can express constraints."}]
cb:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["$","strong",null,{"children":"With schemas, this problem disappears."}]}]
cc:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["No need to say “don’t use the ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"any"}]," type” — if ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"any"}]," doesn’t exist in the schema, the LLM physically cannot generate it. No need to say “don’t create utility functions” — if there’s no slot for utility functions, that’s the end of it. When field types are limited to ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"\"boolean\" | \"int\" | \"double\" | \"string\" | \"uri\" | \"uuid\" | \"datetime\""}]," — 7 choices — there’s no path for the LLM to write ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"\"varchar\""}],"."]}]

116:["$","h2",null,{"id":"6-conclusion","className":"x:tracking-tight x:text-slate-900 x:dark:text-slate-100 x:font-semibold x:target:animate-[fade-in_1.5s] x:mt-10 x:border-b x:pb-1 x:text-3xl nextra-border","children":["6. Conclusion",["$","$L16f",null,{"id":"6-conclusion"}]]}]
117:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"We started at 6.75%. The industry said complex function calling doesn’t work, and our results agreed."}]
118:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"But there was no alternative — deterministic AI output requires structured output — so we built the harness, one failure mode at a time. 119:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"AutoBe achieved 100% compilation across all four Qwen models. Not through better prompts, but through the accumulated engineering of every way things went wrong."}]
11a:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"Three things: type schemas that constrain outputs, compilers that verify results, and structured feedback that corrects errors. These three form a deterministic loop wrapping probabilistic models."}]
11b:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"This pattern is not limited to code generation. The same structure can be built in every engineering domain where deterministic validators exist — semiconductors, chemical processes, control systems."}]
11c:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"Communicate through types and there are no misunderstandings. Constrain through schemas and there are no pink elephants. With a deterministic loop, even 6.75% becomes 100%."}]
11d:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["$","strong",null,{"children":"6.75% is not a failure — it’s the first input to the loop. If you can verify, you converge."}]}]

Here, ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"type"}]," is the discriminator. 128:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"Why does this matter? When an LLM generates data for a union type and makes a mistake, correction requires knowing “which variant was intended.” With a discriminator, identification is simple — check one field and you know the variant. Without one, you must infer intent from the data’s shape — harder, but possible with the right infrastructure."}]

135:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Engineering manages complexity through ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"hierarchical decomposition"}]," — breaking large systems into smaller parts, and those parts into even smaller parts. A chip is blocks, and blocks are sub-blocks. A plant is sections, and sections are units. A building is floors, and floors are rooms. This decomposition forms a tree. At each level, parts have different kinds — blocks can be logic, memory, or IO; units can be reactors, distillation columns, or heat exchangers. ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"DeepPartial<T>"}],": completed fields are valid, and missing fields are a typed object identifiable by schema."]}]

15a:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"This is incremental compilation applied to structured output.
